Informents stated that on 11 Aug 4S between 1200 and 1215 hours, CST,
Jerome and Benno were playing in the back yerd and noticed en object
in descent approximately twelve feet from the ground which was descending
between the two of them. It settled to the ground gently - mich as a
balloon, Upon hitting the ground the object spun once, made a sort of
whistling noise, described by the two boys as resembling a steam whistle,
and then shot straight up into the air approximtely 20 feet, halted
and again made a whistling noise. The object then shop upwerd to a height
of about 30 feet, and, in this ascent, maneuvered around tree branches
and tel me wires. Upon reaching a height o: TO ely ee
objec off in a northeasterly direction. The object approached
from the northwest. —

The boys described the object as "approximately one foot thick, two feet
side and round." The object had no windows, wires, or any visible
appurtenances. It was a dull gray in color and when it hit the ground,
it made a slight clanking noise, mech as metal hitting against metal.

Philip and Mrs Leuer stated the boys were visibly frightened when they
ran into the house and were afraid to tell what they had seen. Upon
close questioning, the boys related the above story to them.

Mr. Leuer advised that hethen had notified Mr. E. R. Sheridan, Postmasteh
at Hemel, Minnesota (who subsequently notified the proper emthorities

woe denen Wresey ve rocks had been leveled. The spot was covered by
a tab.

Object appeared round and probably spherical, At first looked.
similer to weather balloon at about 2000 ft but there was no
characteristic bobbing. The wind was blowing on the ground with

fair strength and gusty yet the object was te steady. In the

time it was under observation (about an hour) it traveled thru a
vertical are of about 25° or mre. After watching the object for

a while the observer concluded that whatever it was the thing wes

at great height but not a star, It had a bluish luminescence like

& fluorescent lamp and as the sun set the color gradually changed to an
orange color at dusk and finally off like a lamp when the
switch is thrown. The outline was c and sharp and symetrical (no
fuzziness or blurring), the air clear with visibility unlimited,
Direction of travel from East directly toward the sun.

Witness saw a luminous, oval-shaped object flying at an altitude of

approx 5,000 ft which was traveling beneath the base of some mimumiz clouds
from southwest to northeast across the sky. The weather was stormy and
cloudy and as a second object flew across the sky a flash of lightning
could be seen thru it. It seemed to be transparent. The objects(3)
traveled very fast and seemed to just disappear within 10 seconds. The objects
appeared at approximately five minute intervals. There was no odor

or sound #@ from them. However, a trail of light about a yard long was
observed. The objects rolled vertically on their edges and appeared to be
spinning en end across the sky. Witnesses lost sight of the objects

as neighboring houses obstructed their view.

SraxkichegxtG, Doyle L. Avery Observer, Atlanta Naval Air Base, Chamblee,

Ga,, while on duty at the observation tower on 26 July 4g about 2100

hours sighted a blue-white light traveling southeast from the northeast

It was traveling very fast and maintained a uniform altitude (undetermined)
and speed. It looked like a shooting star except for the fact that it
maintained the same altitude. While under observation it then appeared

to gain altitude and then turned sharply to the south. The weather was
clear end still. There was no sound, Shortly after sighting the light
several residents of the area called and reported seeing the same berg
No flights left or arrived at the station after 1910 hours on 26 July 4g,

Witnesses of bg eg who were interviewed made statements

as follows: On 26 July 4 they were seated on the lawn of the Georgia
School of Technology Housing Project at Chamblee, Ga, About 2045 hours
Mrs. John Gall sighted a strange light and called it to the attention of
her companions. light appeared about the size of a football and

was traveling southeast on a steady course. It was green and faded into a
silver-colored tail, The light appesred epummmird to lose altitude slowly
much the same as a plane coming in for a landing It appeared to fade
away in flight as if extinguished, No sound washeerd.

A large white ball of fire was observed some four to five miles

directly ahead of and slightly above the aircraft. It was observed

for only a split second and was comparable to the explosion of a shell
were no streamers or "tails" observed such as usually related

to a meteor or a distress signal. As the aircraft approached the

approximate position where the ball of fire was observed a single white

light was seen on the water surface. However, this disappeared within

a few seconds after sighting becemse of the existing cloud coverage.

More lights were immediately observed on the water surface some 10 miles

north of the plane's course. It is the opinion of the witness (6apt

Barnes) that these were from a surface vessel. Some 10 mimutes elapsed

after these lights were observed on the water surface when an aircraft

wes sighted flying a reciprocal course (240°) at about 1000 ft below

the observers's aircraft and slightly to the right of the course.

Capt Barnes made numerous attempts to contact this aircraft on Channel "B"

and "C", "VHF" but acknowledgment of his transmission was never teceived.

floated earthward. While under observation it disintegrated in mid-air.

NOTE: Witness seemed very conscientious and sincere while relating this
incident. Subject arose while witness wes talking to a man from
this Gommand (MCIAXP) when an airplane chanced to fly overhead,

Inci. t: 176

While watching an Aray bomber which was flying rather low, Col Makins
observed above ani going neerly due east an unknown object. He asked

It appeared undulating in shape resembling an ameeba (See sketch inclo<ed).

seemed to be above the main body of the object. Further it appeared that

ancident: 178

Qn the afternoon of 15 October 1948 at approximtely 1705 hours Maj

Robert ©. Drum stated that he had observed a strangely ahaped glistening
object in the north sector of the aky which was moving in a northeasterly
direction at an estimated altitude of 10,000 ft. A stratus layer of clouds
wis situated above the object at an approximate altitude of 14,000 feet.

The disc or discs were moving below thie layer of cloude in that it was noted
that on two occasions that the bighest formation of clouds was at a greater
altitude than the object observed and that the lower cimwd formation blocked
the vision of the observer thus placing the object betwoen the two cl ud
formations or levels. Because of this lower broken cumius clouds at

& Very low altitude wnied wi: toving in s southwesterly direction, tne Major
could not uscertain whether he aaw the same dise four different times or

four different discs. Over a period of 10 mimites the disc or disce were
sighted four times. The longest period of observation was estimate! as 10
seconds, The object or object(s) appeared bright silver in color and circular
or elliptical in shape. No vacor or exhaust war present and no fase or lights
were observed, No sound wae heard, Speed wus estimited as 2.0 MH by using
an estimated 40 4 factor in the swirling build-up of cumlus clouds,

Maj Drum compared the disce with a silver Aollar slightly turned from a flat
osition, givine the object a'disco-shape wien held at arm length at a 46°
angle above eye level. Maj Drum thought that the elliptical effe:t could ve
resulted from the angle at wiich the ob-ery-r and object were located. Maj
Drum etated that he was certain the object was not a conventional cr ft.

Incident: 187

At LO50R, 19 Aug 1948, the Wright-Patterson Plight Service Center

wes notified by Lt Col John Wamgh, Bxecutive Officer, Godman

AP Base, Ky, that an unidentified flying object was visible from

the ground at Godman AFB, The object was ascertained to be at
approximately 30,080 to 40,000 feet altitude, ical in shape,
bright silver color and giving off a bright re ection from the

sun, An F-5l was dispatched from Standiford ANB, Ky, to observe

the object. At 11323 a mocsage received from Godman stated no change
in elevation of the object and reported that it seemed to be moving
‘outhweet from Godman AFB st approximately 235° at an elevation of
60° from Godman AWB, The P-51 was flying at 30,000 to 35,000 ft at
the time and could not locate the object. ‘owever, the object was
visible from the ground with the naked eye and asinmth and elevation
readings were being taken by theodolite every mimite and the progress
of the.object was being charted, At 1219 B19 Ang 1948 Maj

Mitebel, Operntions Officer at Godman ATB, notified Wright-Patterson
Plight Service Center that the unidentified object was ascertained

to be the planet Venus, Thin information wac given the Coumsnding Officer
Godman AFB by Mr Moore, Heed Astrologer. University of Lobisville, Ky.
This assumption was verified by MCI later the same day.

At 1040.A. M. om 3 Nov 48 tuo Mustang fighters of the S24 Fighter
Wing were dispatched to investigate the sighting reported by Lt
Com Pain, a Goast Guard pilot, wav reported the origins) sighting.
The pilots upom recching 22,000 fect sighted the unidentified object.
Tosy kept om climbing until they reached 37,000 feet. They estimated
the object to be another five or six thousand fest above them, At this
height the object resembled am intestine that had been inflated and

it sppe red § to 6 times higher them wide, Sketches vere not available.
Speed was estim ted to Le about 2 mph. Heading apvrozimately 45%/

First report by Strategic Air Command identified the object tentatively
a8 a heavenly body.

Bubsequent information received by Lt Roherkemper, Coast Guard Officer
stationed at Salem identified the craft as a comic ray equipment carrier
used by KIT, Later it ws definitely established that the craft sighted
was a clu ter of eight balloons ued by MIT for comic ray research.

This incident is oomeidered closed in that definite idemtity has been
established,

he had observed jet sirorsft ake tactic.1 approaches at approximately 600

Toe peak of ite arc was approximately 45° above the horizon to the west
southwest of hin position. Altitude was juiged to be 5,000 to 6,00 feet.

Incident 207

Object first sighted at approximately 2145 18 Boy 4S. It was flying from
northeast to southwest across Andrews field at 1700 feet. Plane then
descended from 4,000 ft to same altitude a: object woich was then coming
back across Anirews Field from the Southwest on a Northeast heading.

Object and plane then flew in circles, object on outer circle. Plane followed
it to 7000 ft. While climbing plane mide 3 to 4 passes at the object to
identify it. As plane descended at approximately 240 MPH object would then
climd vertically, drop below plane from benind and continned circling the
field, Yn a last effort the plane switched on = landing light and the
object momentarily gave off a dull glow. It appeared oval with no wings and
no tail surfaces, After the object was sighted in the landing light, it
headed to the Northeast climbing ravidly. Plane lost sight of object at
8,000 ft; the time was then 22:03

Weather at the time: CAVU - no clouds, hase or smoke,

Incident: 217

On 9 December while on flight from Washington, D. C. to Patterson
Field, shortly after leaving Pittsburgh range some & to 12 miles
southeast of Pittsburgh Capt Mulling asked Gol Brow if he were
interested in seeing a flying disc} Col Brow removed his flight
gagles, took a quick look ani said “Looks like a balloon to me"
Capt Mulling didn't agree since the object evinced a very definite
movenent and was proceeding in a southweet direction at a pretty
fair rate of speed. ol Brown then decided to watch the object
again. The object appeared perfectly round and of a chalky white
color bat did not shiny. It was whiter than the clouds and
was seen against . teaeeaa of strips of blue sky alternating with
stripe of thin broken stratus. It was mach whiter than the clouds,
It appeared to be traveling fairly repidly through or above the clouds
disappearing behind them and then reappearing tut gry pursuing

a atreight course. When first sighted it appeared at 60° above

horigon and seemed from the observer's viewpoint to be sbout the size
of a quarter - thought to be about 4 to 5 miles in front of the 0-45
4 borirontal shimmer or jittery motion was noticable at all times.
The wae parm ing a course of 300° and then changed to 270° with
the object one ee on a course of 290°. The C-15 we

at

cruising at 140 6,000 feet. The object anpecred to be 12 to
16,000 feet and was above clouds. Object last seam at sbout 10°
above the corizon and wes about the size of a beebee shot.

Incident: 219

Doris Croke, age 14, a ninth grede student at North Junior High,
Rewburgh, HN. Y., reported that on the night of 29 November 1

at precisely 8:00 while Standing with two young friends on the
corner of Concord St & lat Street, that she observed a ball of fire
with a long tail in the north of the sky. Course of the object
wes from exat to west. It was visible for two seconds and then
went below the horizon, She stated that while she had seen many
shooting stars that she had never seen anything like this. She
also described the astral phenomenon as being a “large red ball
thru the heavens.” che was convinced that she had seen a dlue-tailed
comet. ‘


Under investigation

Incident! ce

At 2220 hours, local time, 23 Bow 4g, Sot Bags Slater saw
an object in the air Qirectly east of Farstenfelibruck at

an unknown altitude, It looked like a rediish star and was
moving in a southerly direction across Munich turning slightly
to the South-West and then South-Mast, Speed could have been
between 200 and 500 MPH. Actual speed could not be estimated.
Capt Slater called Base Operations from his barracks and they
notified Racecard UP Station who reported nothing on the scope
bat st»ted they would look, Raceoard Station then called Base
Operations to report am unidentified object 27,000 ft Up som
30 miles south of Munich, Later Racecard Station called

Capt Slater to report that the object was climbing and was tien
believed to .e at 40,000 ft altitude about 40 wiles south of
Munich and was circling.

There were no F-80's (Jets) flying on this night.

Capt Slater (an F-20 pflet) is considered completely reliable
and the facts st=ted adove were verified by Capt Darwin R. Addis
23rd Fighter Sq (also an P80 pilot) who was with Copt Slater
at the time of the sighting.

Incident: 224

At 1745 on 8 Dec 4S, Spec Agents STAHL & NEYF, both rated pilots, took
off from Kirtland AP Base in a T-7 type aircraft. At 1833 while

flying at an indicated altitude of 11,500 feet, mean sea level,
approximately 5,000 feet above terrain, a strange phenomenon was observed.
Beact position of the aircraft at time of observation wes 35° 3l' Be

104° 51' ¥, approximately 20 miles east of Las Vegas, N. K., radio sonde
station. Aircraft wus on a compass course of a indicated air-speed
wes 160 MPH and ground speed approx 190 “= /A STARL was pilot and
seeted on the left side of cockpit, 3/A KEEP first observed the object
an? a split sec nd later S/A STAFL saw it. It was 2,000 ft bigher than
the plane or 13,500 ft above meen sea level and was approaching

the plane at a ranid rate of speed from approximately 30° to the left

of course, from 60° ENE, to 20° WoW, The object was similar in appearance
to @ burning green flare of*towon use in the Air Forces. dowever, the
light wae much more intense and the object appesred considerably

than a normal flare. No estimate could be made of the distance or size of
the object since no other object was visible won which to base a comarieon.
It was definitely larg r than a shooting star, meteor or flare. The
trajectory of the object when first signted wis almost flat and parallel
to the earth. ‘The listed approximately 2 seconis at the end
of which time the object seemed to burn cut. The trajectory then dropped
off ranidly and a trail of glowing fragments reddish orange in color

wok observed falling toward the ground. There fragments were visible for
lese than a second Defore disappearing. The phenomenon wes of such in-
tensity as to be visible from the very moment it ignited and was observed
a snlit second later. It w.s not possible for the phenomenon to have been
visible for longer than « split’second before observation. (See “e*
under"Significent Differences Between the Fireballs Observed in tre
Interval Dec 5-13 & Typical Meteors" = Incident 227) ,

Incident: 225

Colonel Hayes first observed an umeual aeria) phenomena during the
latter part of 1947. This occurred in the vicinity of Vaugon, Bi, M,
The pheno ema appe..red Spproximately 400-500 ft above the ground

when first obserwed and was descending slowly and steadily in a vertical
manner toward the earth. It appe.red slightly larger than a basketball,
bright white in color like & miniature =m, At a point approximately
200 feet abowe the surface of the exrth, ths object appeared to explode
although no noise was apparent, By this time the Colonel bad stopped
hie automobile and had gotten out to watch the object. The explosion
or disintegrstion appeared to be taking place some 40 to 6) yards
distant and stil) no noise was noticed, At this time the fragnents
assumed a fiery red color and descended toward earth like mauzerous
sparks being extinguished before touctiing the groumd. At the time

Col Hayes was on ighway 60, near a raflroad @nd separated from the
vicinity wnere the fragments were landing by a fence, He did not cross
the fence or investigate further,

ad. In the case of meteorites that penetrate to as low levele as that

“On August 4th, while traveling south on U. 5, Highway No. 30,
approximately two and one-half miles north of North Powder, Oregon,

horizontal line on an angle presumed to be northwest to southeast

type tail, but the object did have a definite fluorescent glow

referring your observation after dark to a nigh tensity neon sign
of green - perhaps on the light green side.*

Mr Tom Rush, a pilot with Dixie Air Service, Jackson, Mississippi,

and his wife (private pilot), signted an unidentified aerial object

2 miles est of Jackson, Mississippi, at 1700 C, 1 Jan 49 while coming
in for a landing at the Dixie Airport. Object was first described es
being cigar-shaped with short etubdby wings resembling a rocket.

(Second renort states there were no wings) Mr Rush specifically stetes

in @ signed letter "as the object made its turn, it wes then that I noticed
the object didn't have wings." Object was first thought to be a large

tow target about 0 feet and sbout 10 feet in diameter at one end

and about 4 ft at the treiling end, The tail even fluttered like a tr rget
wat there was nothing towing it and the object moved at greater speed than
tow targets, When the object crossed in front of the plane Rush estimated
the speed to be about 200 MPH and atsted that the object. crossed sone SOO
sfeet in front of them. After the turn, the object was within 1,200 feet

of the plene, Mr Ruch attempted to point out tbe object to the vilot

but the pilot did not understand and apparently @id not see the ot

As the object turned to the southwest it accelerated its s

out of sight, Object seen for some 10 te’ le seconds,

Dlue or Dlack, Speed at first signting - 200 miles per h

thought to have accelerated to between 400 to 600 mph.

Object wae sighted by the above persons who were pescengers in a
civilian aircreft. No photographs were taken since @ camera wes
ble. The Stinson was flying at about 1,800 feet. Investigation of flight

schedules of odjacent airfields could throw no light on the incident.
